<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What I changed and why
- The dump is a 2 -> 8 -> 3 ReLU MLP with argmax output. Inputs (x0 in [1, 6.9], x1 in [0.1, 2.5])
  look like iris petal length/width, so three ordered classes were expected.
- Instead of simplifying weights neuron by neuron, I evaluated the net on a grid and printed the class
  map. It showed three bands separated by two almost straight, almost parallel lines.
- I bisected along x0 for many fixed x1 to locate each boundary precisely:
  - 0|1 boundary: x0 = 3.262 - 0.984*x1  -> snapped to `x0 + x1 < 3.3`.
  - 1|2 boundary: x0 = 6.755 - 1.083*x1  -> plain `x0 + x1` was too far off at the edges (failed
    with confident disagreements), so the slope was snapped to 11/10: `x0 + 1.1*x1 < 6.75`.
- All 8 hidden neurons and the 3 output scores were replaced by two linear threshold tests in an
  if-chain (the classes are ordered, so a decision list is exact in structure).
- Result: `python check.py agent.py` -> PASS (snap), 0 disagreements where the net is confident.
  3.2 for the first threshold failed; 3.25 and 3.3 both pass, 3.3 gives fewer unsure-zone disagreements.

#### Heuristics
- For low-dimensional argmax nets, map the decision regions on a grid first; the region picture
  tells you the shape of the program (decision list, linear cuts, kinks) far faster than the weights.
- Locate each boundary by bisection at many points along the other input; fit a line, check
  residuals for kinks (none here), then snap slope and intercept.
- Ordered classes in bands -> a chain of `if score < t: return k`, with the last class as the default.
- Try the simplest slope (1) first; if check.py reports confident disagreements clustered at one end
  of the boundary, the slope is wrong, so move to the next simple fraction (here 1.1) rather than
  shifting the threshold.
- Among thresholds that pass, prefer the one closest to the fitted boundary (fewer unsure-zone
  disagreements) while keeping it a round/half-step value.
- Hidden neurons with tiny output weights or that are inactive over the input box (here several
  all-negative-weight neurons with negative bias) are dead code; a region-level rewrite removes them
  all at once.

</details>

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the network does
2 inputs -> 8 ReLU -> 3 logits, argmax. The input ranges (x0 in [1, 6.9], x1 in [0.1, 2.5]) and the
three classes look like the iris petal length / petal width classifier:
class 0 = small petals (setosa), class 1 = medium (versicolor), class 2 = large (virginica).

#### What I changed and why
- Instead of simplifying the 8 hidden units one by one, I mapped the decision regions directly
  (grid print of the class map, then bisection of the class boundary along x0 for many x1 values).
  The map has three bands ordered along x0, separated by two nearly straight boundaries; class 0 and
  class 2 never touch, so a two-test if-chain is enough.
- Boundary 1/2 is straight: x0 = 6.725 - 1.06*x1. Snapping the slope to 1 leaves a drift of
  0.06*x1; the threshold that keeps both ends inside the net's unsure band (half-width ~0.09 in x0)
  is 6.65, giving `x0 + x1 > 6.65`.
- Boundary 0/1 is slightly convex (slope -1.59 at small x1 bending to about -2.1 near x1 = 1.5,
  where it leaves the domain). Fitting one line through it: the slope 7/4 keeps
  x0 + 1.75*x1 within 3.78..3.91 along the whole boundary, so `x0 + 1.75*x1 < 3.85` stays inside the
  unsure band (half-width ~0.11). Slope 2 was too far off (spread 0.39).
- All the hidden-layer arithmetic is gone. Final program:

```python
def net(x0, x1):
    if x0 + x1 > 6.65:
        return 2
    if x0 + 1.75 * x1 < 3.85:
        return 0
    return 1
```

`python check.py agent.py` -> PASS (snap): 0 disagreements where the net is confident; the
remaining disagreements are all inside the net's unsure zone (score < 0.619). PASS (exact) is not
reachable with straight lines because the net's 0/1 boundary is a polyline with several ReLU kinks.

#### Heuristics
- For low-dimensional argmax nets, map the decision regions first (coarse grid print) and then
  locate each boundary precisely by bisection along one input; this is far cheaper than algebra on
  hidden units, most of which only shape the logits away from the boundary.
- Measure the width of the "unsure" band (where top-two logit gap < the checker margin) along each
  boundary; it tells you how much slope/threshold error a snapped line can afford.
- For a nearly straight boundary, snap the slope to a simple value (1, 7/4, 2, ...) and pick the
  threshold as the midpoint of the range that a·x0 + b·x1 takes along the true boundary; accept the
  snap if half that range is below the band half-width, otherwise try the next simple slope.
- If two classes never share a boundary, a short if-chain of linear tests reproduces argmax.
- Recognising the dataset (here iris petal measurements) helps name variables and sanity-check the
  class order, but the network's extrapolation outside the training data still has to be matched,
  because the checker samples the whole input box.

</details>

## max/hand: dump vs agent_1

```python
# dumped from a neural network: 2 inputs -> 3 -> 1 outputs
# inputs: a: float [-3, 3], b: float [-3, 3]
# output: regression: the returned value
def net(a, b):
    h1_0 = relu(1 * a - 1 * b)  # layer 1 neuron 0
    h1_1 = relu(1 * b)  # layer 1 neuron 1
    h1_2 = relu(-1 * b)  # layer 1 neuron 2
    return 1 * h1_0 + 1 * h1_1 - 1 * h1_2
```

```python

    return max(a, b)
```

AST tool (no LLM):

```python

def net(a, b):
    return max(a, b)
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What I changed
- The dump: `relu(x0 - x1) + relu(x1) - relu(-x1)`. All weights were already exact (+-1), no biases.
- `relu(x1) - relu(-x1)` is the identity `x1`, so the net is `relu(x0 - x1) + x1`.
- `relu(a - b) + b = max(a, b)`, so the network computes `max(x0, x1)`.
- Result: `return max(x0, x1)`; `check.py` reports PASS (exact).

#### Heuristics
- A pair of neurons `relu(w.x) - relu(-w.x)` with matching output weights is a linear pass-through: replace it by `w.x`.
- `relu(a - b) + b` = `max(a, b)`; `b - relu(b - a)` = `min(a, b)`; `relu(a) + relu(-a)` = `abs(a)`.
- First collapse pass-through pairs into linear terms, then look for max/min/abs patterns in what remains.
- Check that the simplified algebra is valid over the whole input domain (these identities hold everywhere, so no domain-dependent pruning was needed here).

</details>

```python
def net(a, b, c):
    # median of three = sum minus the largest and the smallest
    return a + b + c - max(a, b, c) - min(a, b, c)
```

AST tool (no LLM):

```python
# produced by nndc ast (route: fit)
def net(a, b, c):
    return max(min(a, b), min(max(a, b), c))
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes: 3 -> 16 -> 16 -> 1 regression network

#### Result
The network computes the **median of three inputs**:

    median(x0, x1, x2) = x0 + x1 + x2 - max(x0, x1, x2) - min(x0, x1, x2)

`python check.py agent.py` gives `PASS (snap)`. The largest error is 0.092 against a snap tolerance of 0.120.
The worst errors sit near the edges of the input box (inputs near +-3) and where two inputs are almost equal.
At those points the trained ReLU net is a rough piecewise-linear fit. It does not follow a different rule there.

#### What I changed and why
- I replaced all 32 hidden ReLU units and the output layer with one closed-form expression. The network
  weights don't snap cleanly one by one: 32 noisy units together approximate a single sorting operation.
  So I found the function from what the network outputs, not by reading its weights.
- How I found it: I sampled 20k uniform inputs and computed a least-squares fit, plus correlations, against
  a set of candidate features (linear terms, products, abs values, pairwise max and |xi - xj|, and the
  max, min and median of all three). The median correlated at r = 0.99999. A linear fit
  `y ~ 1.0007 * median - 0.001` confirmed it: slope 1, intercept 0, so both snap away.
- I wrote the median as sum - max - min so it fits the allowed language (no sort, no median function).
- Exact pass is not attainable in a meaningful way. Matching to 1e-5 would mean copying the network's
  approximation error, which is the noise this task asks me to remove.

#### Heuristics
- Find out what the network computes before you try to simplify its weights. For a small number of
  inputs, sample the input box and regress the output on a set of candidate features: linear terms,
  products, abs, pairwise max/min, |xi - xj|, and order statistics (max, min, median). A correlation
  near 1 with one feature tells you what the network computes.
- Symmetric behaviour (equal coefficients on every input, equal pairwise max terms) suggests a
  permutation-invariant function: sum, mean, max, min, median, count of positives, and so on.
- Hidden-layer weights that come in near-opposite pairs (for example +0.73*x1 - 0.73*x2, or
  -0.78*x0 + 0.77*x1) are pairwise comparisons `xi - xj`. Those are the building blocks of max, min and sorting.
- After you name a candidate, fit `y ~ a*f + b`. Then snap a and b to simple values (here 1 and 0) and
  check the largest error against the tolerance.
- Rewrite order statistics with the allowed primitives. For 3 inputs: median = sum - max - min. In general,
  the k-th order statistic can be built from nested min/max.
- In regression tasks the remaining errors gather at the edges of the input domain and on ties
  (xi close to xj), where a small ReLU net can only approximate a kink. If the errors are confined to
  those places, it's approximation noise and not a sign that the formula is wrong.

</details>

```python
def net(a, b, c):
    # median of the three inputs
    return max(min(a, b), min(max(a, b), c))
```

AST tool (no LLM):

```python
# produced by nndc ast (route: fit)
def net(a, b, c):
    return max(min(a, b), min(max(a, b), c))
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the network computes
The 3 -> 16 -> 16 -> 1 ReLU net is an approximation of the **median of the three inputs**.
`agent.py` replaces all 32 hidden neurons with the exact closed form

    median(x0, x1, x2) = max(min(x0, x1), min(max(x0, x1), x2))

`check.py` result: PASS (snap), max |program - net| = 0.109 against a snap tolerance of 0.120.
The remaining error is the net's own approximation error. It is largest near the corners of the
input box, e.g. (3, 3, -3), where the net gives 2.89 instead of 3. A program that matches exactly
would have to copy that error, so I kept the clean formula.

#### How it was found
- Layer 1 weights come in near +/- pairs on input differences: x2-x0 (neurons 3, 8, 14 and 4 with
  the opposite sign), x0-x1 (0) and x2-x1 (1). Other neurons follow the sum x0+x1+x2 with both signs
  (7/12, 10/11). Pairwise differences plus a sum point to an order statistic or another symmetric
  function of the inputs.
- I sampled the real network (via `weights.npz`) on 20k uniform points. Then I fit `a*f(X)+b` for a
  list of candidate functions: max, min, median, sum, mean, range, products, norms and a linear model.
  Median gave slope 0.998 and intercept -0.016, with RMS residual 0.008. Every other candidate had
  residuals above 0.5, so median was the clear winner. With the slope snapped to 1 and the intercept
  to 0, the formula passes.

#### Heuristics
- Look at layer 1 before anything else. Neurons with weights like (+a, -a) on two inputs compute
  differences or comparisons, and paired neurons with opposite signs (relu(z) and relu(-z)) build
  |z| or a z = relu(z) - relu(-z) identity. These patterns suggest sorting, min/max or abs.
- Don't simplify the network weight by weight. Treat it as a black box: sample it and fit a library
  of simple candidates (linear, min/max/median, abs, products, norms, range) with a free scale and
  offset. A slope near 1 and an offset near 0 then confirm the candidate.
- Compare the residual against the checker's tolerance (here 2% of the output range). A small net
  approximating a piecewise-linear target makes its largest errors at the corners and edges of the
  input box. Expect the worst-case error there, and don't add correction terms only to imitate
  those errors.
- Write order statistics with nested min/max. For example, median3 = max(min(a,b), min(max(a,b),c)).
- Snap any fitted scale or offset to 1 or 0 when the result stays inside tolerance. The clean formula
  is the intended function, and the remaining gap is the network's training error.

</details>

```python
def net(a, b, c):
    # median of the three inputs
    return max(min(a, b), min(max(a, b), c))
```

AST tool (no LLM):

```python
# produced by nndc ast (route: fit)
def net(a, b, c):
    return max(min(a, b), min(max(a, b), c))
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the network computes
The 3 -> 16 -> 16 -> 1 ReLU net is an approximation of the **median of the three inputs**.
`agent.py` replaces all 32 hidden neurons with

    median(x0, x1, x2) = max(min(x0, x1), min(max(x0, x1), x2))

Check: `PASS (snap)`, max |program - net| = 0.052 against a snap tolerance of 0.12. An exact pass
(1e-5) is not reachable with a clean formula: the residual is the net's own approximation
error (largest near the corners of the domain, e.g. two inputs near -3, where the net is off by ~0.05),
not a missing term.

#### How it was found
1. Sampled 200k uniform points in [-3,3]^3 and evaluated the reference float32 forward pass.
2. Linear fit: coefficients ~0.4 on each input, equal -> output is a symmetric function of the inputs,
   but residual was large (1.77), so it is piecewise.
3. Fit on a library of symmetric piecewise-linear features (|xi|, |xi-xj|, pairwise max, max/min of
   all three, products, squares). Products/squares/|xi| got ~0 weight; pairwise |xi-xj|, pairwise
   max, global max and min carried everything -> an order statistic.
4. Tested median directly: slope 0.998, intercept -0.001, max error 0.05 -> median, coefficients
   snapped to 1 and 0.

#### Heuristics
- Start with a least-squares fit on raw inputs; equal coefficients on all inputs signal a symmetric
  function (sorting / order statistics, sum, mean, norm).
- Build a feature library of candidate piecewise-linear primitives (abs, pairwise max/min, |xi-xj|,
  global max/min) plus a few nonlinear ones (products, squares). Features that get ~0 weight can be
  eliminated; the surviving set names the function family.
- Order statistics of n inputs: linear coefficient ~ 1/n-ish on each, and the fit concentrates on
  max/min/|xi-xj| features. Median of 3 = sum - max - min = max(min(a,b), min(max(a,b),c)).
- Once a candidate is guessed, regress the net's output on it (slope, intercept): slope ~1 and
  intercept ~0 confirm it and give the snapped coefficients.
- Look at the worst residuals: if they sit at the domain corners / edges (where training data is sparse)
  and are small relative to the output range, they are approximation error of the net, not structure
  worth reproducing. Don't chase exact pass by encoding that error.
- Read the checker's tolerances first (here snap tol = 2% of output range) to know how much
  approximation error is acceptable.

</details>

## neareq/hand: dump vs agent_1

```python
# dumped from a neural network: 2 inputs -> 2 -> 1 outputs
# inputs: a: float [-2, 2], b: float [-2, 2]
# output: binary: class 1 if the returned value > 0, else class 0
def net(a, b):
    h1_0 = relu(1 * a - 1 * b)  # layer 1 neuron 0
    h1_1 = relu(-1 * a + 1 * b)  # layer 1 neuron 1
    return -1 * h1_0 - 1 * h1_1 + 0.2
```

```python
def net(a, b):
    # class 1 when a and b are within 0.2 of each other ("approximately equal")
    tolerance = 0.2
    return tolerance - abs(a - b)
```

AST tool (no LLM):

```python
# produced by nndc ast (route: structural-exact)
def net(a, b):
    return 0.2 - abs(a - b)
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What changed and why
- The dump has two hidden ReLUs, `relu(x0 - x1)` and `relu(x1 - x0)`, both added to the output with weight -1.
  Since relu(d) + relu(-d) = |d|, the hidden layer collapses to `abs(x0 - x1)`.
- The output is `0.2 - |x0 - x1|`, so class 1 means "x0 and x1 are within 0.2 of each other"
  (an approximate-equality test). The bias 0.2 is the named constant `tolerance`.
- Weights were already ±1. No snapping was needed. `check.py` reports PASS (exact). The only
  disagreements are float rounding at the exact boundary |x0 - x1| = 0.2, which the checker
  puts in the unsure zone.

#### Heuristics
- A pair of ReLUs with opposite weight vectors (`relu(w.x + b)` and `relu(-w.x - b)`) and equal
  output weights is `abs(w.x + b)`. Unequal output weights give a piecewise-linear "leaky" form.
- A bias minus an absolute difference, compared to 0, is a band test: |a - b| < c. If the sign
  is reversed, it is an "inputs differ by more than c" test.
- Keep the real-valued score, not just a boolean, so the check can pass exactly rather than
  only by snapping.
- Before snapping anything, look for algebraic identities (abs, min, max) among the hidden units.
  They often remove the whole hidden layer.
- Boundary disagreements of about 1e-16 are float rounding, not logic errors.

</details>

```python
# dumped from a neural network: 2 inputs -> 8 -> 1 outputs
# inputs: a: float [-2, 2], b: float [-2, 2]
# output: binary: class 1 if the returned value > 0, else class 0
def net(a, b):
    h1_0 = relu(-3.7059202 * a + 3.7096167 * b - 0.26511204)  # layer 1 neuron 0
    h1_1 = relu(-1.691698 * a + 1.3458985 * b + 1.62702)  # layer 1 neuron 1
    h1_2 = relu(-0.13582842 * a + 0.050595496 * b - 0.6135267)  # layer 1 neuron 2
    h1_3 = relu(-3.2976904 * a + 3.3075483 * b - 0.24522409)  # layer 1 neuron 3
    h1_4 = relu(-3.5263665 * a + 3.5303345 * b - 0.2563369)  # layer 1 neuron 4
    h1_5 = relu(5.160562 * a - 5.1673737 * b - 0.26749894)  # layer 1 neuron 5
    h1_6 = relu(-1.4279348 * a + 0.96105784 * b + 1.458404)  # layer 1 neuron 6
    h1_7 = relu(-1.8917894 * a + 2.1996834 * b + 1.7307748)  # layer 1 neuron 7
    return -9.288122 * h1_0 + 1.5636504 * h1_1 + 0.19832896 * h1_2 - 9.440439 * h1_3 - 9.7136135 * h1_4 - 10.776145 * h1_5 + 1.2490262 * h1_6 + 3.661764 * h1_7 + 0.0032594635
```

```python
def net(a, b):
    # class 1 iff the two inputs are approximately equal
    tolerance = 0.2
    diff = b - a
    return abs(diff) <= tolerance
```

AST tool (no LLM):

```python
# produced by nndc ast (route: boundary-scan)
def net(a, b):
    d0 = a - b
    return abs(d0) < 0.2
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the network does
Class 1 iff `|x1 - x0| <= 0.2` ("x0 and x1 are approximately equal").

#### What I changed and why
- Every hidden neuron except h1_2 has weights of the form `a*(x1 - x0) + b` (coefficients on x0 and x1
  are equal and opposite), so the only real feature is `diff = x1 - x0`.
- h1_2 (`-0.136*x0 + 0.051*x1 - 0.61`) is negative everywhere on [-2,2]^2 -> always 0 -> removed.
- h1_0, h1_3, h1_4 are three copies of the same unit `~3.5*relu(diff - 0.07)` with large negative output
  weights (~-9.5 each): a steep penalty for diff > 0. h1_5 `~5.2*relu(-diff - 0.05)` with weight -10.8
  is the mirror penalty for diff < 0. h1_1, h1_6, h1_7 are broad positive "bias" units that are active
  over the whole region near the diagonal and contribute ~+11.5 there.
- Result: a ridge along x0 == x1. Scanning the decision boundary along lines of constant x0+x1 gives
  diff in about [-0.2028 - 0.0006*s, 0.2030 - 0.0009*s] -- a symmetric band of half-width ~0.2 with a
  negligible tilt. Snapped to the single shared threshold 0.2.
- Checked: `PASS (snap)`, 0 disagreements where the net is confident; all residual disagreements are in
  the thin sliver 0.1997 < |diff| < 0.2063 (the net's boundary is slightly tilted/asymmetric).
  `PASS (exact)` would require reproducing that tilt, i.e. essentially the raw weights, so I kept the clean
  formula.

#### Heuristics
- Look for neurons whose input weights are (nearly) equal and opposite: they depend only on a difference
  (or with equal signs, a sum) of inputs. Re-express the whole net in that derived variable.
- Bound each pre-activation over the input box (min/max via weight signs); a ReLU that is never positive
  is dead and can be dropped, one never negative is linear.
- Neurons with proportional weight vectors (same direction, similar bias/scale ratio) are duplicates;
  merge them by summing output weights times scale.
- Large negative output weights on relu(+d - c) and relu(-d - c) pairs, plus positive "bias" units,
  form a band/ridge detector: output > 0 iff |d| < threshold. Find the threshold numerically by scanning
  the decision boundary, then snap to a round number.
- For binary outputs, measure the boundary directly (bisection/scan along the derived variable for
  several values of the orthogonal variable) rather than reasoning about every unit's magnitude;
  if the boundary barely moves, it is a constant threshold.
- Prefer returning a comparison (1/0) for binary outputs; the score's magnitude doesn't matter.

</details>

## neareq/seed1: dump vs agent_1

```python
# dumped from a neural network: 2 inputs -> 8 -> 1 outputs
# inputs: a: float [-2, 2], b: float [-2, 2]
# output: binary: class 1 if the returned value > 0, else class 0
def net(a, b):
    h1_0 = relu(3.4741066 * a - 3.4732788 * b - 0.3514232)  # layer 1 neuron 0
    h1_1 = relu(-4.277891 * a + 4.272618 * b - 0.40335733)  # layer 1 neuron 1
    h1_2 = relu(-3.8758993 * a + 3.8697417 * b - 0.38936132)  # layer 1 neuron 2
    h1_3 = relu(-3.9580228 * a + 3.952065 * b - 0.40076992)  # layer 1 neuron 3
    h1_4 = relu(3.2914214 * a - 3.2902577 * b - 0.33740592)  # layer 1 neuron 4
    h1_5 = relu(0.55146116 * a - 0.54878926 * b + 2.6810098)  # layer 1 neuron 5
    h1_6 = relu(3.6162682 * a - 3.6150029 * b - 0.3641312)  # layer 1 neuron 6
    h1_7 = relu(-0.051554263 * a - 0.06365895 * b - 0.3050039)  # layer 1 neuron 7
    return -5.871051 * h1_0 - 7.1336207 * h1_1 - 4.15549 * h1_2 - 8.786397 * h1_3 - 9.063077 * h1_4 + 2.6034918 * h1_5 - 11.070301 * h1_6 + 0.33536497 * h1_7 + 1.5788696
```

```python
def net(a, b):
    # class 1 iff a and b are within 0.2 of each other
    diff = a - b
    return 0.2 - abs(diff)
```

AST tool (no LLM):

```python
# produced by nndc ast (route: boundary-scan)
def net(a, b):
    d0 = a - b
    return abs(d0) < 0.2
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the network does
Class 1 iff |x0 - x1| < 0.2, i.e. the two inputs are almost equal. The output is `0.2 - abs(x0 - x1)`.

#### How I got there
- Every live hidden neuron has weights of about (+a, -a) or (-a, +a), so each one depends only on d = x0 - x1.
- Neurons 0, 4, 6 are about a*relu(d - 0.1), with a around 3.3 to 3.6. Neurons 1, 2, 3 are about a*relu(-d - 0.1).
  All six use the same hinge at |d| = 0.1, so they act as one hinge per side.
- Neuron 5 (0.55*d + 2.68) is always positive on the input box (|d| <= 4 gives at least 0.48), so it is linear.
  It adds to the bias: about 8.56 + 1.44*d.
- Neuron 7 is always 0 on the box (its maximum is about -0.07), so it is dead and I removed it.
- Collapsed output: 8.56 + 1.44d - 90.3*relu(d - 0.1) - 81.4*relu(-d - 0.1).
  Its zeros are at d = +0.198 and d = -0.202, so I snapped both to |d| = 0.2. The slight
  asymmetry is training noise, and `0.2 - |d|` has the same sign everywhere.
- Only the sign matters for a binary output, so I could replace the steep piecewise-linear
  score with the simple `0.2 - |d|`.
- Result: `PASS (snap)`. The only disagreements are points exactly on |d| = 0.2. There the net is
  unsure, and its own boundary there is not symmetric in x0 and x1, so no simple formula could
  match it exactly.

#### Heuristics
- Look for neurons whose weights are opposite in sign and nearly equal in size. They read a difference (x0 - x1), so rewrite them with a new variable `d`.
- Divide each neuron by its weight size to get relu(d - t). Neurons with almost the same t and same direction can be merged into one hinge, with their output weights times their sizes added together.
- Use the input bounds to check each neuron's preactivation range. If it is always positive, the neuron is linear: fold it into the bias and the linear terms. If it is always negative, it is dead: remove it.
- For a binary output, solve for where the collapsed score is zero, then snap those crossing points (here +-0.198 and -0.202 become +-0.2). Then write the simplest score with the same sign, for example `t - abs(d)` for a band.
- Two hinge groups that mirror each other around 0, with a positive bias, usually mean a band `|d| < t` (an "almost equal" test).
- Disagreements exactly on the snapped boundary are expected. Accept them when the checker says they are in the unsure zone.

</details>

## neareq/seed2: dump vs agent_1

```python
# dumped from a neural network: 2 inputs -> 8 -> 1 outputs
# inputs: a: float [-2, 2], b: float [-2, 2]
# output: binary: class 1 if the returned value > 0, else class 0
def net(a, b):
    h1_0 = relu(3.4995902 * a - 3.5024092 * b - 0.357402)  # layer 1 neuron 0
    h1_1 = relu(0.13386506 * a + 0.023924395 * b - 0.47717747)  # layer 1 neuron 1
    h1_2 = relu(0.9971862 * a - 0.07908688 * b + 1.4944394)  # layer 1 neuron 2
    h1_3 = relu(1.0029399 * a - 1.3183084 * b + 2.7881167)  # layer 1 neuron 3
    h1_4 = relu(3.4161203 * a - 3.4211483 * b - 0.3481083)  # layer 1 neuron 4
    h1_5 = relu(3.1720812 * a - 3.1756787 * b - 0.33471)  # layer 1 neuron 5
    h1_6 = relu(-3.9738536 * a + 4.000095 * b - 0.35588154)  # layer 1 neuron 6
    h1_7 = relu(-4.09036 * a + 4.1099324 * b - 0.37514275)  # layer 1 neuron 7
    return -9.06185 * h1_0 + 0.033111986 * h1_1 + 1.0269562 * h1_2 + 2.2614715 * h1_3 - 9.315151 * h1_4 - 10.154277 * h1_5 - 8.680973 * h1_6 - 10.320841 * h1_7 + 1.3724438
```

```python
def net(a, b):
    # class 1 iff the two inputs are close: |a - b| < 0.2
    return 0.2 - abs(a - b)
```

AST tool (no LLM):

```python
# produced by nndc ast (route: boundary-scan)
def net(a, b):
    d0 = a - b
    return abs(d0) < 0.2
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the network computes
Class 1 iff the two inputs are nearly equal: `|x0 - x1| < 0.2`.

`agent.py`:
```python
def net(x0, x1):
    return 0.2 - abs(x0 - x1)
```
`python check.py agent.py` -> `PASS (snap)`, 0 disagreements where the net is confident
(all differences lie in the net's own unsure zone near the boundary).

#### Analysis / what I changed
- Hidden neurons 0, 4, 5 are all `relu(k*(x0 - x1) - ~0.35)` with k ~ 3.2..3.5, i.e. the same
  hinge `relu(x0 - x1 - ~0.1)` scaled; all have large negative output weights (~ -9..-10).
  Combined: about `-96 * relu(x0 - x1 - 0.1)`.
- Neurons 6, 7 are the mirror: `relu(x1 - x0 - ~0.09)` with combined weight about -77.
- Neuron 1 (`0.134*x0 + 0.024*x1 - 0.477`) is negative on the whole input box -> dead, removed.
- Neurons 2, 3 plus the bias form a small positive "budget" P (about 2..20, ~9 near the diagonal)
  that is always > 0. Inside the band `|x0-x1| < ~0.1` the output is P > 0 -> class 1; outside,
  the steep hinges overpower P. The crossing sits at `|d| ~ 0.1 + P/96 ~ 0.2`.
- I measured the actual boundary by sweeping along the diagonal (x0 = m + d/2, x1 = m - d/2):
  crossings at d in [-0.213, -0.197] and [0.200, 0.213] for m in [-2, 2]. The slight drift with m
  comes from P depending on x0 and is noise around one shared threshold, snapped to 0.2.
- So everything collapses to a symmetric band of half-width 0.2 around the diagonal.
  An exact pass would need reproducing the slight x-dependent tilt of the boundary, which is
  an artifact of the training, not part of the intended function.

#### Heuristics
- Normalize each hidden neuron as `relu(scale * (direction . x - threshold))`: neurons with the
  same direction (up to sign/scale) and close thresholds are duplicates; merge them by summing
  `out_weight * scale`.
- Check each neuron's pre-activation range over the input box (interval arithmetic: sum of
  |w|*range); neurons that are never positive are dead, ones never negative are linear.
- Pairs of mirrored hinges `relu(d - t)` and `relu(-d - t)` with large negative weight plus a
  positive bias mean "|d| < t'": a band / closeness test, i.e. `t' - abs(d)`.
- Small-weight, always-positive neurons combined with the bias are often just a "positive
  offset" that shifts the threshold; estimate threshold = hinge_offset + offset / hinge_slope,
  or measure the decision boundary numerically along the relevant direction.
- For binary outputs, find the boundary empirically (sweep along the discovered direction at
  several positions) and snap the crossings to one shared round threshold; small drift across
  the domain is usually training noise.
- Directions with coefficients like (3.50, -3.50) or (-3.97, 4.00) are just +-(x0 - x1);
  look at coefficient ratios, not magnitudes.
- Try the simplest formula first with the checker; only add detail if confident points fail.

</details>

## ramp/hand: dump vs agent_1

```python
# dumped from a neural network: 1 inputs -> 2 -> 1 outputs
# inputs: x: float [-2, 3]
# output: regression: the returned value
def net(x):
    h1_0 = relu(1 * x)  # layer 1 neuron 0
    h1_1 = relu(1 * x - 1)  # layer 1 neuron 1
    return 1 * h1_0 - 1 * h1_1
```

```python
def net(x):
    # relu(x) - relu(x - 1) is x clamped to [0, 1]
    return min(max(x, 0), 1)
```

AST tool (no LLM):

```python
# produced by nndc ast (route: structural-exact)
def net(x):
    return min(max(x, 0), 1)
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

The dump is `relu(x0) - relu(x0 - 1)` with all weights already exactly ±1 and bias -1.
That difference of two shifted ReLUs with equal slopes is a saturating ramp:
0 for x0 <= 0, x0 on [0, 1], 1 for x0 >= 1. So the whole net is `clamp(x0, 0, 1)`,
written as `min(max(x0, 0), 1)`. No snapping was needed. Check: PASS (exact).

#### Heuristics

- `relu(x - a) - relu(x - b)` with a < b and equal unit slopes is `clamp(x, a, b) - a`,
  i.e. a ramp that saturates; replace with `min(max(x, a), b) - a`.
- A sum of ReLUs of the same 1-D input is a piecewise-linear function; locate the kinks
  (where each pre-activation is 0) and the slope change at each to read off the shape.
- If weights are already clean integers, look for a closed form (clamp, abs, max/min)
  rather than just simplifying arithmetic: `relu(x) + relu(-x) = abs(x)`,
  `relu(x) - relu(-x) = x`, `a + relu(b - a) = max(a, b)`.
- Check the input bounds: kinks outside the input range can be dropped.

</details>

## sparse8/hand: dump vs agent_1

```python
# dumped from a neural network: 8 inputs -> 1 -> 1 outputs
# inputs: u0: float [0, 1], u1: float [0, 1], u2: float [0, 1], u3: float [0, 1], u4: float [0, 1], u5: float [0, 1], u6: float [0, 1], u7: float [0, 1]
# output: regression: the returned value
def net(u0, u1, u2, u3, u4, u5, u6, u7):
    h1_0 = relu(0.0005 * u0 + 0.0005 * u1 + 1 * u2 + 0.0005 * u3 + 0.0005 * u4 - 1 * u5 + 0.0005 * u6 + 0.0005 * u7)  # layer 1 neuron 0
    return 1 * h1_0
```

```python
def net(u0, u1, u2, u3, u4, u5, u6, u7):
    # positive part of the difference u2 - u5; other inputs are unused
    return relu(u2 - u5)
```

AST tool (no LLM):

```python
# produced by nndc ast (route: structural-snap)
def net(u0, u1, u2, u3, u4, u5, u6, u7):
    return max(0, u2 - u5)
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What I changed and why

- The dump is a single ReLU neuron: `relu(x2 - x5 + 0.0005*(x0+x1+x3+x4+x6+x7))`, output scaled by 1.
- The six 0.0005 weights contribute at most 6 * 0.0005 = 0.003 over the [0,1] input box, well under the
  snap tolerance (0.02), so they were treated as training noise and snapped to 0.
- Weights +1 / -1 on x2 / x5 kept as is; the output scale of 1 was folded away.
- Result: `return relu(x2 - x5)`, i.e. the positive part of the difference x2 - x5 (a "how much does x2 exceed x5" feature).
  `check.py` gives `PASS (snap)`, max deviation 0.003, all within the snap/unsure zone.
- Keeping the 0.0005 terms would reproduce the net exactly, but they carry no meaning, so I preferred the clean formula.

#### Heuristics

- Bound each small weight's total contribution by |w| * (input range) summed over inputs; if that sum stays below
  the snap tolerance, drop those terms entirely.
- Weights within a few percent of 0, ±1 or small integers are almost always the intended values; snap them.
- Fold identity output layers (weight 1, bias 0) and single-neuron hidden layers straight into the return expression.
- A single ReLU of a signed difference `relu(a - b)` means "excess of a over b"; recognize common motifs
  (relu(a-b) + b = max(a, b), relu(a-b) - relu(b-a) = a - b, etc.) when there are more neurons.
- Inputs whose weights are all snapped to zero are unused arguments; keep them in the signature only.
- Verify with the checker after each snap; if failures appear, look at the listed inputs to see which snapped term mattered.

</details>

## sum8/hand: dump vs agent_1

```python
# dumped from a neural network: 8 inputs -> 1 -> 1 outputs
# inputs: v0: int [0, 255], v1: int [0, 255], v2: int [0, 255], v3: int [0, 255], v4: int [0, 255], v5: int [0, 255], v6: int [0, 255], v7: int [0, 255]
# output: regression: the returned value
def net(v0, v1, v2, v3, v4, v5, v6, v7):
    h1_0 = relu(0.02 * v0 + 0.02 * v1 + 0.02 * v2 + 0.02 * v3 + 0.02 * v4 + 0.02 * v5 + 0.02 * v6 + 0.02 * v7 - 20)  # layer 1 neuron 0
    return 1 * h1_0
```

```python
def net(v0, v1, v2, v3, v4, v5, v6, v7):
    total = sum(v0, v1, v2, v3, v4, v5, v6, v7)
    # amount by which the 8-input total exceeds 1000, in units of 50
    return relu(total - 1000) / 50
```

AST tool (no LLM):

```python
# produced by nndc ast (route: structural-exact)
def net(v0, v1, v2, v3, v4, v5, v6, v7):
    return max(0, 0.02 * sum(v0, v1, v2, v3, v4, v5, v6, v7) - 20)
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What I changed and why
- The network is one ReLU neuron followed by an identity output (weight 1, bias 0), so the output
  layer was dropped.
- All 8 input weights are identical (0.02 = 1/50, verified exactly in weights.npz), so the weighted
  sum factors into `sum(x0..x7) / 50`.
- The bias -20 is a threshold of 20 * 50 = 1000 on the raw total. So
  `relu(0.02*total - 20) == relu(total - 1000) / 50`. The output is "how far the sum of the 8 bytes
  goes above 1000, scaled by 1/50" (the maximum total is 2040, so the output ranges 0..20.8).
- Result: `PASS (exact)` (max error ~5e-6, float32 rounding).

#### Heuristics
- Read the raw weights file, not only the dumped code, to confirm whether coefficients are exactly
  "nice" (e.g. 0.02 = 1/50) or just printed rounded.
- Identity layers (weight 1, bias 0) and single-neuron chains collapse; fold them away.
- When all weights of a neuron are equal, factor them out: `w * sum(inputs) + b`.
- Rewrite `relu(w*s + b)` with w > 0 as `relu(s + b/w) * w` so the threshold `-b/w` is expressed
  in input units (here 1000) and becomes interpretable; snap that threshold to a round number or
  a half-step between integers.
- Prefer dividing by an integer constant (/50) over multiplying by a decimal (0.02) for exactness
  and readability.
- Compute the reachable input range (here total in [0, 2040]) to check whether a ReLU is ever
  inactive/always active; if one side is unreachable, the ReLU can be removed.

</details>

```python
def net(income):
    h1_0 = relu(-0.00748682 * income - 0.088744044)  # layer 1 neuron 0

    h1_2 = relu(-0.82304513 * income - 0.30221307)  # layer 1 neuron 2
    h1_3 = relu(-0.735939 * income - 0.19656539)  # layer 1 neuron 3
    h1_4 = relu(-0.38515437 * income - 0.9553485)  # layer 1 neuron 4
    h1_5 = relu(0.13552383 * income - 10.826945)  # layer 1 neuron 5
    h1_6 = relu(-0.01981318 * income - 0.4122231)  # layer 1 neuron 6
    h1_7 = relu(0.6629604 * income - 1.8950348)  # layer 1 neuron 7
    return 0.13977209 * h1_0 + 0.30971268 * h1_1 - 0.23968837 * h1_2 - 0.15395936 * h1_3 + 0.12841663 * h1_4 + 1.4832989 * h1_5 - 0.07276136 * h1_6 + 0.18404423 * h1_7 + 0.06626776
```

```python
def net(income):
    # Convex piecewise-linear ramp: flat 0 below 3, then slopes 1/8, 1/5, 2/5
    # with kinks at income = 3, 21.5 and 80.
    return 0.125 * relu(income - 3) + 0.075 * relu(income - 21.5) + 0.2 * relu(income - 80)
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What I changed and why

- Input domain is x0 in [0, 200]. Neurons 0, 2, 3, 4, 6 have a negative slope and a negative
  bias, so their pre-activation is < 0 for every x0 >= 0: they are always 0 and were removed.
- The three live neurons are ramps `w*relu(a*x0 + b)`. Rewrote each as `(w*a) * relu(x0 - k)`
  with kink `k = -b/a`: kinks 2.858, 21.554, 79.890; slopes 0.1220, 0.0769, 0.2010.
- Cumulative slopes are 0.122, 0.199, 0.400 -> snapped to 1/8, 1/5, 2/5 (increments 0.125,
  0.075, 0.2). Kinks snapped to 3, 21.5 (half-step), 80. Output bias 0.066 snapped to 0
  (it is ~0.1% of the 62-unit output range).
- Result: `0.125*relu(x0-3) + 0.075*relu(x0-21.5) + 0.2*relu(x0-80)`, a convex
  progressive-rate (tax-bracket-like) ramp. Max error 0.084 vs snap tolerance 1.24 -> PASS (snap).
  PASS (exact) would require keeping the raw constants, which defeats the purpose.

#### Heuristics

- Use the input bounds to find dead ReLUs: if sign(a*x+b) is fixed over the whole domain the
  unit is either always 0 (drop) or always linear (fold into the output affine part).
- In 1-D, normalise each unit to `slope * relu(x - kink)`; kinks = -b/a, slopes = w_out*a.
  The function is then a piecewise-linear spline; read it as segments.
- Snap cumulative slopes (the slope of each segment) to simple fractions rather than snapping
  the individual increments; the segment slopes are what the original rule most likely used.
- Snap kinks to integers or half-steps; small output biases relative to the output range snap to 0.
- Check the snap tolerance (fraction of output range) first to know how aggressive snapping can be,
  and verify numerically on a dense grid before running the checker.

</details>

```python
    return 0.22146748 * h1_0 - 0.0014123917 * h1_1 + 4.2832613 * h1_2 + 0.28131846 * h1_3 - 3.5517135 * h1_4 - 0.030627983 * h1_5 - 0.059261084 * h1_6 + 0.0273881 * h1_7 - 0.39553866
```

```python
    return max(0.18 * income - 1, 0.4 * income - 18.16)
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the network computes
1 input (x0 in [0, 200]) -> 8 ReLU units -> 1 linear output. With one input every hidden unit is
just a hinge at x0 = -b/w, so the whole net is a 1-D piecewise-linear curve.

- Dead units: h1_1 and h1_6 have negative slope and negative bias, so they are 0 on the whole
  domain [0, 200]. Removed.
- Hinges of the remaining units: 0.89 (h1_5), 1.62 (h1_7), 2.00 (h1_0), 2.06 (h1_3), 78.0 (h1_2),
  196.4 (h1_4). The four hinges in 0.9..2.1 cluster around x0 = 2 and carry tiny slope changes
  except h1_0/h1_3, so they were merged into one kink at x0 = 2.
- Resulting segments (slope): x0<2: about -0.13; 2..78: 0.185; 78..196: 0.400; 196..200: 0.314.
- The curve is convex except for the last little kink at 196 (an edge artifact near the domain
  boundary, error 0.19 if ignored), so it is written as a max of three lines:
  `max(-0.5 - x0/8, 0.185*x0 - 1.12, 0.4*x0 - 18)`.
  Intercepts were snapped (-0.488 -> -0.5, -17.96 -> -18, slope -0.13 -> -1/8); the lines still
  cross at x0 = 2 and x0 ~ 78.

Result: `PASS (snap)`, max |error| = 0.19 against a snap tolerance of 1.25 (worst at x0 = 200,
where the dropped kink would have mattered). Exact pass would require keeping all raw
coefficients, which defeats the purpose.

#### Heuristics
- For a 1-input net, compute each unit's hinge point -b/w and its contribution to the slope
  (output weight * input weight); the net is then a list of (kink, slope delta) pairs.
- Drop units that are inactive over the whole input range (sign of w and b both keep the
  pre-activation negative on [lo, hi]).
- Merge hinges that are close together into one kink; drop hinges whose slope delta is tiny.
- Hinges very close to the domain edge with small effect are usually fitting artifacts; check the
  error from removing them against the tolerance.
- If the slopes are increasing along the axis the function is convex: write it as max(line, ...)
  (decreasing slopes -> min). This removes all relu terms and makes intercepts readable.
- Snap slopes/intercepts to simple values (1/8, 0.4, integers) and re-verify; the check's snap
  tolerance (2% of output range here) tells how much freedom there is.

</details>

```python
def net(income):

    h1_6 = relu(-0.022080746 * income + 4.607337)  # layer 1 neuron 6
    h1_7 = relu(-0.05077642 * income + 4.045834)  # layer 1 neuron 7
    return 0.39947325 * h1_0 - 0.20857231 * h1_1 + 0.12130789 * h1_2 + 0.6281135 * h1_3 - 0.10892576 * h1_4 + 0.20019028 * h1_5 - 3.468607 * h1_6 + 3.9647968 * h1_7 - 0.17204256
```

```python
def net(income):
    # Tiered (progressive) rate on income: 0.1 per unit up to 20, 0.2 per unit from 20 to 80,
    # 0.4 per unit above 80.  Cumulative: 2 at income=20, 14 at income=80, 62 at income=200.
    tier1 = min(income, 20)
    tier2 = min(relu(income - 20), 60)
    tier3 = relu(income - 80)
    return 0.1 * tier1 + 0.2 * tier2 + 0.4 * tier3
```

AST tool (no LLM):

```python
# produced by nndc ast (route: spline)
def net(income):
    return max(0.12 * income, 0.2 * income - 1.68, 0.4 * income - 17.68)
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the network computes
A 1 -> 8 -> 1 ReLU net on x0 in [0, 200] is a piecewise-linear function of one variable. I listed
each hidden unit's kink (x = -b/w) and whether it is active inside the domain, then summed
`w_out * w_in` per segment to get the slope on each interval.

- h1_1 (-0.238*x - 0.885) is never active for x >= 0: removed.
- h1_6 kinks at x = 208.7 > 200, so it is linear on the whole domain (folds into slope/offset).
- Kinks at 0.08, 0.55 and 0.94 (h1_5, h1_0, h1_4) only bend the curve slightly near 0 (output
  about -0.1 there): fitting noise around a zero start.
- Kinks at 20.4 and 21.6 (h1_3, h1_2) are one breakpoint: snapped to 20.
- Kink at 79.7 (h1_7) snapped to 80.

Segment slopes: about 0.12 on [1, 20], 0.20 on [21.6, 79.7], 0.40 above 80, which is a
progressive tier schedule with rates 0.1 / 0.2 / 0.4 (doubling) and breakpoints 20 and 80.
Values check out: net(20)=2.16 (2), net(80)=14.16 (14), net(200)=62.3 (62).

#### Result
`f = 0.1*min(x,20) + 0.2*min(relu(x-20),60) + 0.4*relu(x-80)`
(equivalently `0.1*x + 0.1*relu(x-20) + 0.2*relu(x-80)`).
check.py: PASS (snap), max error 0.30 against a tolerance of 1.25 (largest at x=200, where the
net's top slope is 0.401 instead of 0.4).

#### Heuristics
- For a 1-D input, turn the net into a list of kinks (-b/w per hidden unit) and segment slopes;
  that is the whole function.
- Drop units whose kink lies outside the input range on the inactive side (always zero), and fold
  units whose kink lies outside on the active side into the linear term.
- Merge kinks that are close together (20.4 and 21.6) into one round breakpoint; treat a cluster of
  kinks near the domain edge with tiny net effect as fitting noise around the boundary.
- Read slopes as rates: look for simple ratios between segments (here each doubles) and round to
  simple decimals.
- Check key points (breakpoints, ends) against round numbers to confirm the intercept (here 0).
- Write the result as tiers (min/relu clamps) so the business meaning (tiered pricing/tax) shows.
- The snap tolerance is a fraction of the output range; a small slope error times a long
  interval is where the largest deviation shows up (the domain end).

</details>

```python
    # triangular "hat" bump centred at 3 with half-width 1: 0 outside [2, 4], peak 1 at x = 3
    return relu(1 - abs(x - 3))
```

AST tool (no LLM):

```python
# produced by nndc ast (route: structural-exact)
def net(x):
    return max(0, 1 - abs(x - 3))
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What I changed and why
- The dump is 1 -> 3 -> 1: three ReLUs `relu(x0 - k)` with knots k = 2, 3, 4 and output weights
  +1, -2, +1. That is a second difference of a ramp, i.e. a piecewise-linear hat (triangle) function:
  slope 0 for x0 < 2, +1 on [2, 3], -1 on [3, 4], 0 again for x0 > 4 (slopes sum 1 - 2 + 1 = 0).
- Rewrote it as `relu(1 - abs(x0 - 3))`: a triangular bump centred at 3, half-width 1, height 1.
- No snapping was needed; the weights were already integers. Check: PASS (exact).

#### Heuristics
- For a 1-D input, read each hidden ReLU as a knot at -bias/weight and the output as a sum of slope
  changes; accumulate slopes left to right to get the piecewise-linear shape.
- Output weights of the pattern (+a, -2a, +a) on equally spaced knots k-d, k, k+d form a hat:
  `a*d*relu(1 - abs(x - k)/d)`. Patterns (+a, -a) on two knots form a clamp/saturating ramp
  (`a*min(max(x - k1, 0), k2 - k1)`).
- If the slopes sum to zero the function is constant beyond the last knot - check the tail value to
  decide whether a bump (returns to 0) or a step (plateau) is meant.
- Prefer abs/min/max formulations over raw ReLU sums: they make centre, width and height explicit.

</details>


AST tool (no LLM):

```python
# produced by nndc ast (route: structural-exact)
def net(x):
    return 0.5 - abs(x)
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What changed and why
- The dump has two hidden ReLUs, `relu(x0)` and `relu(-x0)`, each with output weight -1, plus a bias of +0.5.
- `relu(x) + relu(-x) == abs(x)`, so the two neurons together compute `-abs(x0)`. With the bias added, the network is `0.5 - abs(x0)`.
- Meaning: class 1 when `|x0| < 0.5`, which is a band of width 1 centred on 0 (an "is x0 near zero" detector). The weights were already clean (±1, 0.5), so nothing needed snapping.
- `check.py` reports `PASS (exact)`. The only disagreements are at x0 = ±0.5 exactly, where the net's score is about 1.5e-8 (float32 noise on the boundary), inside the unsure zone.

#### Heuristics
- A pair of ReLUs with opposite input weights (`relu(w*x+b)`, `relu(-w*x-b)`) and equal output weights is `abs(w*x+b)` times that output weight. Collapse it into one `abs`.
- A pair with opposite input weights and opposite output weights is just linear: `relu(z) - relu(-z) == z`.
- For a binary output, write the score in a form whose sign is easy to read, like `c - abs(x - m)`. It reads directly as "within c of m", and you can name the centre and the half-width.
- Before snapping, check whether the weights are already exact simple values. If they are, aim for `PASS (exact)` with an algebraically equivalent formula rather than a rewritten decision rule.
- Disagreements that sit exactly on the decision boundary with near-zero scores are float noise, not errors in the formula.

</details>

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the network does
Binary classifier on one input x0 in [-2, 2]: class 1 iff |x0| < 1/2 (a band around zero).

#### What I changed and why
- `h1_0 = relu(-0.0075*x0 - 0.385)`: pre-activation is negative for every x0 in [-2, 2]
  (max is -0.37), so the neuron is always 0. Removed (dead neuron).
- The output is `6.086 - 6.87*relu(7.72*x0 - 3.00) - 4.83*relu(-6.0*x0 - 2.55) - 6.99*relu(-6.8*x0 - 2.85)`:
  a constant positive bias minus ramps that switch on to the right (h1_1) and to the left
  (h1_2, h1_3). The two left ramps have nearly the same kink (-0.4245 vs -0.4187) and just
  add up to one steeper ramp.
- Since only the sign matters, I solved for the zero crossings numerically:
  x0 = -0.5005 and x0 = 0.5031. Both snap to ±1/2, symmetric, so the decision is
  `0.5 - abs(x0) > 0`.
- Result: `PASS (snap)`; the only disagreements are in the thin slivers between ±0.5 and the
  network's true crossings (inside the unsure zone). `PASS (exact)` would require keeping the
  unsnapped thresholds -0.5005 / 0.5031, which carry no meaning.

#### Heuristics
- First check each hidden neuron's pre-activation range over the input box; neurons that are
  never positive are dead and can be deleted (and always-positive ones are linear).
- For a binary output, the magnitude of the score is irrelevant: find the decision boundary
  (zero crossings of the piecewise-linear score) and re-express it directly, rather than
  snapping individual weights.
- In 1-D, a piecewise-linear net is fully described by its kinks; between kinks solve the
  linear equation for the zero crossing.
- Several ReLUs with nearly equal kink points and the same sign of slope act as one ramp; merge them.
- A positive bias minus ramps going both ways = an interval/band; if the two boundaries are
  roughly opposite, express it with abs() (|x - c| < r).
- Snap boundaries to the nearest simple value (here ±0.5, i.e. a half-step) and accept
  disagreements that fall only inside the network's low-confidence zone.

</details>


AST tool (no LLM):

```python
# produced by nndc ast (route: boundary)
def net(x):
    return abs(x) < 0.5
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the network does
1 input -> 4 ReLU -> 1 binary output. It's a "small magnitude" detector: class 1 iff |x0| < 1/2.

#### What I changed and why
- **Dead neurons removed.** h1_0 = relu(0.426*x0 - 1.030) only fires for x0 > 2.42, and
  h1_2 = relu(-0.060*x0 - 0.336) only fires for x0 < -5.59. Neither can fire on the domain [-2, 2],
  so both contribute 0 and were deleted.
- **Mirror pair recognised.** h1_1 = relu(-8.03*x0 - 3.20) and h1_3 = relu(8.00*x0 - 3.09) have
  nearly opposite slopes and similar biases; both have output weight about -6.3. Together they are
  about -6.3*relu(8*|x0| - 3.15): a symmetric "V" penalty around 0 subtracted from the bias +5.52.
- **Decision boundaries solved.** With output = 5.52 - 6.3*(8|x0| - 3.15) on the active side, the
  zero crossings are x0 = -0.5077 (left) and x0 = +0.4965 (right). Both snap to +/-1/2, one shared
  threshold.
- **Simplified to a formula.** Only the sign of the output matters, so the whole net is
  `0.5 - abs(x0)` (positive -> class 1). The scale is irrelevant for a binary output.
- Result: `PASS (snap)`. All disagreements are within ~0.008 of +/-0.5 (the net's unsure zone).
  Exact agreement would require keeping the asymmetric unsnapped thresholds (-0.5077, 0.4965),
  which is training noise rather than meaning, so I kept the snapped version.

#### Heuristics
- First compute each hidden unit's activation threshold (-b/w for 1-D inputs, or the bias range
  over the input box) and delete units that are never (or always, then linearise) active on the
  domain. Small output weights (|w| << other weights) are another hint of a vestigial unit.
- Look for pairs of units with opposite-sign slopes of similar magnitude and similar biases and
  equal output weights: relu(a*x - b) + relu(-a*x - b) = relu(a*|x| - b) when b >= 0, i.e. an
  abs-value feature.
- For binary outputs, don't snap individual weights first; solve for where the output crosses the
  threshold, then snap those crossing points (here +/-0.5), and rewrite as a simple signed margin
  (e.g. `c - abs(x)`). Overall scale of the output is irrelevant.
- When two boundaries are nearly symmetric (-0.508 vs 0.496), merge them into one shared threshold
  and use abs().
- Ignore large magnitudes inside units (slopes of 8): steep ReLUs are just the network's way to
  make a sharp step; what matters is where the step happens.

</details>

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What I changed and why

- Neuron h1_3 = relu(-0.051*x0 - 0.808): on x0 in [-2, 2] its pre-activation is at most
  0.102 - 0.808 < 0, so it is always 0. Removed it.
- h1_0 and h1_2 have nearly the same kink (x0 = 0.4164 and 0.4207) and both feed the output with
  negative weight, so they act as one ramp "x0 > ~0.42". h1_1 is the mirror ramp "x0 < ~-0.40".
- Solved for the zero crossings of the output exactly: right edge x0 = 0.50046, left edge
  x0 = -0.50043. Between them all ramps are 0 and the output is the positive bias 5.68.
  So the net says class 1 iff -0.5 < x0 < 0.5, i.e. |x0| < 1/2.
- Final program: `return 0.5 - abs(x0)`. check.py: PASS (snap); the only disagreements are
  in a ~0.0005-wide sliver beyond +/-0.5 inside the net's unsure zone. Getting PASS (exact)
  would need the unsnapped edges 0.50046 / -0.50043, which carry no meaning.

#### Heuristics

- For each ReLU, compute its pre-activation range over the input bounds; drop neurons that are
  never active (or never inactive -> they are linear and fold into the bias/other terms).
- Group neurons whose kinks (x = -b/w) are close and whose output weights share a sign; they
  behave as a single ramp.
- For binary outputs only the decision boundary matters: solve output = 0 piecewise per linear
  region rather than simplifying the score itself, then snap the boundaries.
- A positive bias plus negative-weighted ramps on both sides of 0 is a "band" detector:
  rewrite as `c - abs(x - m)` with snapped centre m and half-width c.
- Mirror-symmetric boundaries (+0.5004 / -0.5004) strongly suggest abs().
- Accept PASS (snap) rather than encoding the noisy exact boundaries.

</details>

## xor/hand: dump vs agent_1

```python
# dumped from a neural network: 2 inputs -> 2 -> 1 outputs
# inputs: p: bool [0, 1], q: bool [0, 1]
# output: binary: class 1 if the returned value > 0, else class 0
def net(p, q):
    h1_0 = relu(1 * p + 1 * q)  # layer 1 neuron 0
    h1_1 = relu(1 * p + 1 * q - 1)  # layer 1 neuron 1
    return 1 * h1_0 - 2 * h1_1 - 0.5
```

```python
def net(p, q):
    # XOR of two bools, as a margin: +0.5 if exactly one input is set, else -0.5
    return abs(p - q) - 0.5
```

AST tool (no LLM):

```python
# produced by nndc ast (route: truth-table)
def net(p, q):
    return p != q
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the network does
Two bool inputs, hidden layer `h0 = relu(x0+x1)`, `h1 = relu(x0+x1-1)`, output `h0 - 2*h1 - 0.5`.
With s = x0 + x1 in {0,1,2}: h0 = s, h1 = max(s-1, 0), so the output is -0.5, +0.5, -0.5.
That is XOR, with a constant margin of 0.5.

#### What I changed
- Replaced the two ReLUs with `abs(x0 - x1) - 0.5`. On {0,1}^2, `abs(x0 - x1)` equals
  `s - 2*relu(s-1)`, so the score matches the network exactly (PASS exact, exhaustive over 4 inputs),
  not only the class.
- The weights were already snapped (1, -1, -2, -0.5), so no snapping was needed.

#### Heuristics
- With bool inputs and few of them, list every input and the raw score; the truth table often
  names the function (here XOR) straight away.
- If a hidden neuron sees only a sum s of inputs, rewrite the network as a piecewise-linear
  function of s. `relu(s) - 2*relu(s-1)` is a "tent" shape, which is `1 - abs(s-1)`, and on bools
  is `abs(x0 - x1)`.
- When the score takes only two values (±c), write it as `indicator - c` with a closed-form
  indicator (abs, min, max) so the result matches exactly, not just in sign.
- A bias of -0.5 on a 0/1-valued expression is the usual "half-step" threshold: the output is
  `expr > 0.5`, i.e. `expr == 1` for integers.

</details>

```python
# output: binary: class 1 if the returned value > 0, else class 0

    h1_1 = relu(-3.401813 * p - 3.4014056 * q + 3.4014304)  # layer 1 neuron 1
    h1_2 = relu(-0.27234524 * p + 0.1896159 * q - 0.2136969)  # layer 1 neuron 2
    h1_3 = relu(-2.825353 * p + 2.8253255 * q - 0.0000149551815)  # layer 1 neuron 3
    return -5.299233 * h1_0 - 6.0862217 * h1_1 - 0.20611155 * h1_2 + 4.350323 * h1_3 + 9.590704
```

```python
def net(p, q):

    return p != q
```

AST tool (no LLM):

```python
# produced by nndc ast (route: truth-table)
def net(p, q):
    return p != q
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the network does
Two boolean inputs, one hidden layer of 4 ReLUs, binary output (class 1 if score > 0).
Evaluating all 4 input combinations of the dump:

| x0 | x1 | score  | class |
|----|----|--------|-------|
| 0  | 0  | -11.1  | 0     |
| 1  | 0  | +9.59  | 1     |
| 0  | 1  | +11.9  | 1     |
| 1  | 1  | -11.4  | 0     |

That is XOR, so `agent.py` is simply `return x0 != x1` (comparison gives 1/0). PASS (exact), exhaustive.

#### What the hidden units were doing (for the record)
- h1_0 ~ relu(2*x0 + 4*x1 - 2): active when x1 = 1 (penalty for "x1 and maybe x0").
- h1_1 ~ 3.4 * relu(1 - x0 - x1): NOR detector, fires only at (0,0), pushes score negative.
- h1_2: weights ~0.2-0.27 with negative bias; it is 0 on every boolean input -> dead, removed.
- h1_3 ~ 2.83 * relu(x1 - x0): "x1 and not x0", boosts (0,1) back positive.
- Bias 9.59 makes (1,0) positive by default.
Note h1_0 at (1,0) and h1_1 at (1,0) sit exactly at the ReLU kink (pre-activation ~0), which
is why exact coefficients don't matter once snapped.

#### Heuristics
- With a small discrete input domain (here 2 bools = 4 points), enumerate every input and read
  off the truth table first; the decision rule is often a textbook boolean function (XOR, AND, majority).
- For binary/argmax outputs only the sign/ordering matters; the score magnitude can be dropped, so a
  comparison expression (returning 1/0) is a valid replacement for a whole weighted sum.
- Units whose pre-activation is negative on every reachable input are dead; delete them.
- Biases that equal minus one weight (e.g. 2.08*x0 - 2.08) put the ReLU kink exactly on an input
  corner; snap them to integer thresholds like relu(x0 + ... - 1).
- Recognize standard ReLU motifs: relu(1 - a - b) = NOR, relu(a + b - 1) = AND, relu(b - a) = b AND NOT a
  for booleans.
- Verify with the checker after each simplification; prefer the simplest formula that passes exact.

</details>

## xor/seed1: dump vs agent_1

```python
# dumped from a neural network: 2 inputs -> 4 -> 1 outputs
# inputs: p: bool [0, 1], q: bool [0, 1]
# output: binary: class 1 if the returned value > 0, else class 0
def net(p, q):
    h1_0 = relu(3.209216 * p - 3.1135607 * q + 0.037852257)  # layer 1 neuron 0
    h1_1 = relu(-3.029829 * p + 3.2472088 * q + 3.0290747)  # layer 1 neuron 1
    h1_2 = relu(-2.8020875 * p + 2.8020873 * q + 0.000002432534)  # layer 1 neuron 2
    h1_3 = relu(-4.805323 * p + 4.8053827 * q - 0.000023159055)  # layer 1 neuron 3
    return 4.2488623 * h1_0 - 3.6241052 * h1_1 + 3.2231588 * h1_2 + 5.986145 * h1_3 - 1.8743557
```

```python
def net(p, q):
    # XOR of the two boolean inputs: class 1 iff exactly one input is set
    return p != q
```

AST tool (no LLM):

```python
# produced by nndc ast (route: truth-table)
def net(p, q):
    return p != q
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What I changed and why

The dump is a 2 -> 4 -> 1 ReLU network on two boolean inputs with a binary output (class 1 if > 0).
With only 4 possible inputs I evaluated the network on the whole truth table:

| x0 | x1 | score (approx) | class |
|----|----|----------------|-------|
| 0  | 0  | -12.7          | 0     |
| 1  | 0  | +11.9          | 1     |
| 0  | 1  | +13.2          | 1     |
| 1  | 1  | -13.1          | 0     |

That is XOR. The hidden units are recognisable pieces of it:
- h1_0 ~ 3.2*relu(x0 - x1): "x0 and not x1".
- h1_2, h1_3 ~ 2.8*relu(x1 - x0), 4.8*relu(x1 - x0): duplicate "x1 and not x0" detectors (redundant; merge into one).
- h1_1 ~ 3*relu(1 - x0 + x1): a bias-carrying unit that pushes the (0,0) and (1,1) cases negative.
- Output bias -1.87 is just a margin term.

Snapped form would be roughly `relu(x0 - x1) + relu(x1 - x0) - something`, i.e. |x0 - x1|, which for
booleans is exactly `x0 != x1`. Since the output is binary, only the sign matters, so the whole net
collapses to `return x0 != x1` (1 for class 1, 0 for class 0, and 0 is not > 0). Checker: PASS (exact),
exhaustive over all 4 inputs.

#### Heuristics

- When the input space is small (few booleans / small integer ranges), enumerate it fully and read off
  the truth table first; that often identifies the function directly (XOR, AND, majority, ...).
- For a binary output only the sign matters: drop scale factors and replace the score with a boolean
  expression that has the same sign pattern (comparisons yield 1/0, and 0 maps to class 0).
- Hidden units with weights (+a, -a) on two inputs and ~0 bias are relu(x_i - x_j) "i and not j"
  detectors; several units with proportional weight vectors are duplicates and can be merged by
  summing their output weights.
- relu(a - b) + relu(b - a) = |a - b|; on booleans |a - b| == (a != b).
- A unit with a large positive bias that is always active acts as an affine term (bias + linear
  correction), not a real nonlinearity; fold it into the output bias/linear part.
- Tiny biases (1e-5 .. 0.04) relative to weights of ~3 are noise: snap them to 0.

</details>

## xor/seed2: dump vs agent_1

```python
# dumped from a neural network: 2 inputs -> 4 -> 1 outputs
# inputs: p: bool [0, 1], q: bool [0, 1]
# output: binary: class 1 if the returned value > 0, else class 0
def net(p, q):
    h1_0 = relu(-0.1083648 * p + 0.015899912 * q + 0.26979038)  # layer 1 neuron 0
    h1_1 = relu(0.19390899 * p - 0.036117494 * q - 0.6260332)  # layer 1 neuron 1
    h1_2 = relu(-0.35209712 * p + 0.21288416 * q - 0.41265488)  # layer 1 neuron 2
    h1_3 = relu(-0.08127111 * p - 0.57166946 * q - 0.2532823)  # layer 1 neuron 3
    return 0.000004500076 * h1_0 + 0.40884846 * h1_1 - 0.30302483 * h1_2 - 0.09293491 * h1_3 - 0.000001158528
```

```python
def net(p, q):
    # class 1 exactly when p is false (q is ignored): NOT p
    return 1 - 2 * p
```

AST tool (no LLM):

```python
# produced by nndc ast (route: truth-table)
def net(p, q):
    return not p
```

<details><summary>notes</summary>

### Notes

#### What the network computes
Two bool inputs, binary output (class 1 if value > 0). Evaluating all 4 inputs:

| x0 | x1 | active hidden units | output |
|----|----|--------------------|--------|
| 0 | 0 | only h1_0 (0.270) | +5e-8 -> 1 |
| 0 | 1 | only h1_0 (0.286) | +1.3e-7 -> 1 |
| 1 | 0 | only h1_0 (0.161) | -4.3e-7 -> 0 |
| 1 | 1 | only h1_0 (0.177) | -3.6e-7 -> 0 |

- h1_1, h1_2, h1_3 have pre-activations that are negative on every corner of {0,1}^2
  (e.g. h1_1 max = 0.194 - 0.626 < 0), so they are dead and removed.
- The only live unit h1_0 is always positive, so the network is affine:
  out = 4.5e-6 * (0.270 - 0.108 x0 + 0.016 x1) - 1.16e-6. Its sign flips on x0 only
  (the x1 term is too small to cross zero), i.e. the decision is `not x0`.
- The whole output scale is ~1e-7, below the checker's atol, so the net is technically "unsure"
  everywhere; still, the sign pattern is clean and consistent, so I kept the net's classes.

#### Final program
`return 1 - 2 * x0`  -> +1 for x0=0, -1 for x0=1 (symmetric margin around the 0 threshold).
check.py: PASS (exact), 0 disagreements (a constant would also "pass" via ties, but disagrees on 2 inputs).

#### Heuristics
- For small discrete (bool) input spaces, enumerate every input and read the truth table directly;
  that beats snapping weights one by one.
- Check each ReLU's max pre-activation over the input box (sum of positive weights * hi + bias);
  if it is <= 0 the unit is dead and can be dropped. If its min is >= 0 the ReLU is the identity.
- When all live units are always-on, the net collapses to one affine function; then only the sign
  pattern (for classification) matters, and tiny coefficients that never change a sign can be dropped.
- For binary outputs, rescale the final score to small integers (e.g. +-1) with a margin around the
  threshold rather than reproducing tiny raw magnitudes.
- Beware of a checker that treats near-zero scores as ties: a trivial program can "pass"; confirm
  zero disagreements, not just the PASS label.

</details>
